## Preface to the Third Edition

It should perhaps be made clear that the primary purpose of this Manual has always been that alluded to in the title: to set out the standard norms of transcription used by the Dictionary of the Old Spanish Language for the benefit of those many scholars who kindly provide texts as grist to the Dictionary mill. This volume is not, then, intended as a Manual of Spanish Paleography in the broader sense. The plates are chosen with an eye to illustrating certain features of manuscript texts which require special coding for machine-readable Dictionary input; they are not designed to provide a representative sample of different hands from various periods. It is therefore, on the one hand, gratifying that this book has in earlier editions reached beyond the audience at which it was directed; and on the other, saddening to note that there is currently no up-to-date introduction to Spanish paleography to fill an obvious need.
David Mackenzie

## Preface to the Fourth Edition

Because of its commitment to improving its computer-based methodology, particularly as advances in technology make such changes more cost-effective, the Dictionary of the Old Spanish Language Project has introduced some minor changes in its transcription procedures since the publication of the Third Edition of this manual. The conversion to a microcomputer-based operation has allowed some welcome simplification in the coding which was necessary when concordances were produced on a mainframe computer. The Fourth Edition introduces these modifications, along with some innovations (e.g., new language mnemonics) and clarifications of some of the policies laid out in previous editions. While the structure of the Third Edition remains essentially unaltered, a conscious effort has been made to include more examples to further illuminate the procedures, and to this end additional plates from manuscripts and incunabula (with accompanying transcriptions) have been incorporated into this Fourth Edition.
Victoria A. Burrus

## Preface to the Fifth Edition

Since the fourth edition of this manual was published, work has continued on the transcription of a wide variety of texts undertaken to provide the input for the Dictionary of the Old Spanish Language. The combination of a desire to solve new issues raised by the editing experience of many scholars, and the need to represent textual features not previously considered, has led to the revision and publication of this Fifth Edition. In some areas, information has been added; in others, discussion has been clarified in the hope that the manual will become a more efficient tool for its users. Several new plates and transcriptions have been incorporated in order to illustrate new material and further exemplify existing procedures. Editors familiar with previous editions should pay special attention to the following sections, which are substantially altered or newly included:
0. Identification

### 3.211 Alphabet


### 3.212 Numerals


### 3.213 Special Characters


### 3.214 Punctuation


### 3.215 Diacritics


### 3.221 Word Separation

### 3.224 Insertions: Scribal and Editorial


### 3.225 Deletion-Insertion Combinations

This list is not exhaustive: users will encounter minor changes in other sections. In order to facilitate consultation of specific points, an Index has been included for the first time and will be found at the end of the Norms of Transcription. It is my pleasure to thank many editors for their suggestions; especially valuable observations were offered by Ivy Corfis, Charles Faulhaber, and Martha E. Schaffer. The advice received from my colleague John Nitti is reflected on every page. All responsibility for errors, omissions, and shortcomings, however, remains my own.
Ray Harris-Northall

## Introduction

The Dictionary of the Old Spanish Language culls its lexical items from manuscripts transcribed by many contributing scholars; to ensure uniformity it was decided to adopt conservative editorial criteria. The manuscript is transcribed line by line, respecting the manuscript line divisions, so that a concordance may be generated on the basis of folio and line number; this permits a rapid check of the original. An attempt is made to represent the format of the manuscript page. However, the transcription, though close, is not paleographic, and certain norms—imposed by the limitations of the ASCII character set reproduced at the end of this volume—have been established which will be set out in this Manual. The Dictionary relies heavily on the computer to eliminate much drudgery in the sorting of words, and special codes—mnemonics—have been developed to make sure that the sorting is accurate, and that a word may be immediately traced to its exact location in the manuscript: the use of these mnemonics will also be explained. Mnemonics are also used to signal the presence of certain special features, such as Rubrics, Miniatures, Diagrams and the like.
The manual is divided into two parts:

## A. NORMS OF TRANSCRIPTION


## B. EXAMPLES

The two parts should be used in conjunction, following the indications in Part A of examples represented in the plates reproduced in Part B. It is strongly recommended that before setting out on a transcription project, the user peruse both the Manual in its entirety and the text to be transcribed. Initial inspection of the text should focus on scribal practices which will directly influence editorial decisions, such as the representation of palatal /—/ (Section 3.215b), regular word separation and conjunction (Section 3.221), or scribal abbreviation and expansion (Section 3.222).
A. NORMS OF TRANSCRIPTION

## 0. IDENTIFICATION


### 0.1 Norms of transcription are laid out so as to present items as they might occur on a typical folio side. The following information, insofar as it is known and available, should appear at the beginning of the text, before the transcription, and before any indication of foliation or other physical features. The information should be inserted in place of the italic text in the following illustration, and must end with a period before the braces are closed. No punctuation other than the period may be used immediately before the closing brace, and no period may appear anywhere else within any of the fields. Note that on the third and fourth lines, the three pieces of information are delimited by a single vertical bar with one space on each side (see the transcriptions of Plates 23 and 44).

```text
{RMK: Author.}
{RMK: Title.}
{RMK: City | Printer | Date.}
{RMK: City | Library | Shelfmark.}
```

The third line contains imprint information for printed texts only; the fourth line, with current location of the text, should be used for both manuscripts and printed texts. Note that in each case the braces must be opened and closed on the same line: information should not run over onto a second line. If no information is available for any one of the remark lines, that line should be omitted entirely. For other uses of the {RMK:} mnemonic, see Section 5.

## 1. FOLIATION


### 1.1 The first element in the transcription of a folio side is the indication of the folio number itself. The notation used is fol., followed by a space and an Arabic numeral which is the folio number, followed immediately by either the letter r or v, all of which is enclosed in brackets, thus: [fol. 1r]. This appears alone on the first line of the transcription of each folio side. For an example see Section B, Plate 1, line 1. The line reference is to the transcription accompanying the plate; line numbering in increments of five appears in the left margin. (Henceforth, examples will be cited by plate number and line number, thus: 1/1.) Line numbers are included in order to facilitate access to the referenced examples only. Editors should make no attempt to insert line numbers in their own transcriptions.


### 1.2 Folio Sequence: the editor should impose his own folio numeration, using Arabic numerals and starting with the first folio of the codex, which he will call 1r. Any manuscript foliation which is at variance with his numeration should be ignored. If a codex contains more than one manuscript, the numeration should run straight through the codex, and each manuscript should be numbered as part of the codex, not as a separate entity.


### 1.21 Therefore, manuscripts and incunabula having incorrect or non-sequential foliation should be renumbered in folio sequence from the start of the codex.


### 1.22 Guard sheets are not normally included in the foliation sequence since they are not usually considered to be part of the codex. However, if a guard sheet contains a reconstruction of text which had originally been on the first folio or folios which were subsequently lost, that guard sheet should be included in the foliation sequence; it is folio 1r/v and initiates the sequence.


### 1.23 A blank folio side found in a quire which also contains folio sides bearing text must be included in folio enumeration; however, only the folio indication is transcribed for such a blank folio side. Thus, in the following example, folio 2r is blank:

```text
[fol. 1v]
text of 1v
[fol. 2r]
[fol. 2v]
text of 2v
```


### 1.24 Folio or page numbers which are not used in transcription (incorrect or old foliation, foliation in Roman numerals, etc.) may, if desired, be retained in the heading (see Section 2.6).


### 1.3 If it is determined that the loss of one or more folios has caused a break in logical sense, or non-sequitur, in the text, that break in logical sense should be indicated by inserting [—] at the bottom of the folio side before the break. This marker should be placed on a separate line, after a catchword (see Section 4), if a catchword appears on the folio side which precedes the lost folio(s):

```text
{CW. ca. & priega}
```

[—]   (2/82-83)
Since the foliation indication refers only to extant folios, the total loss of a folio does not affect the numbering sequence. (See Section 3.224d(1) for additional uses of the non-sequitur marker.)

## 2. HEADING


### 2.1 A running title, or head, is often found at the top of a folio side, and may be, for example, the title of the chapter or of the book; this is referred to as the heading, and it is transcribed on a separate line of text immediately below the folio indication, within the brace-bounded mnemonic {HD.}, thus:

```text
{HD. LEVITICUS}
```

For examples, see 5/2, 11/2, 26/2, 28/2. Note that HD is always enclosed in braces, as are all mnemonics (see Section 3.213). Within the braces, the heading mnemonic, HD, is followed immediately by a period. The period is followed by one space, and the text of the heading is then inserted, in such a way that both mnemonic and text are enclosed within the braces. The only exception occurs when an editorial remark or comment on the heading is deemed necessary; see Section 5, Remark Fields, for specifics relating to editorial remarks.
Occasionally the heading might appear at the bottom of the folio side, and it is transcribed on a separate line of text immediately below the closing of the {CB.} mnemonic, within the brace-bounded mnemonic {HD.} (e.g., 48/96).

### 2.2 The heading sometimes occupies the top portion of two facing folio sides, starting on a verso and finishing on the following recto. In such cases, since the heading is not complete on either of the folios, the initial segment of the heading contained on the first (verso) folio side is followed by a single space and a plus sign (+) in order to indicate that the heading will be continued on the next (facing) folio side. The plus sign must be placed before the closing brace of a brace-bounded mnemonic, see Section 3.213. The second segment of the heading, which concludes it on the second folio side, is transcribed simply as it appears in the text (e.g., 30/2 and 31/2).


### 2.21 If a heading is complete on only one folio side, no plus sign appears within the brace-bounded heading mnemonic (e.g., 5/2).


### 2.3 A word within a heading may itself be divided across two folio sides  (see Plates 3 and 4 for an example). In this case, the divided word should be transcribed in its entirety within the first section of the heading, with the division indicated by means of a hyphen (AR-GENT, 3/2). As described above, the word is followed by a single space and a plus sign, indicating the continuation of the heading in the next {HD.} mnemonic. See Section 3.221d(5) for an explanation of the use of the hyphen with square brackets in the heading.


### 2.31 If the concluding segment of the divided word in a heading is the only heading text present on a folio side, a heading mnemonic containing no text (line 6 of the following example) must be inserted to mark the physical location in the manuscript of that concluding word segment.

```text
[fol. 1v]
{HD. ALE-XANDRE +}
{CB1.
text of 1v}
[fol. 2r]
{HD.}
{CB1.
text of 2r}
```


### 2.4 A heading which consists of two or more lines which cross folio sides (e.g., 6 & 7/2-3) also requires special treatment. Note that in this case only one {HD.} mnemonic appears on each folio side.

The two incomplete lines of folio side 108v end with the plus sign to indicate that they are continued in the next like mnemonic. The two lines of the heading as it appears in the manuscript are preserved in the transcription of 109r.
```text
[fol. 108v]
{HD. GENE-SIS +
```

L<IBRO> +}
```text
text of 108v
[fol. 109r]
{HD.
```

IX}
```text
text of 109r
```


### 2.5 Occasionally separate headings appear above each of the columns on a folio side. In such instances an Arabic numeral is inserted after {HD.} to indicate the specific column of a multi-column folio side to which a heading refers (e.g., 4/2-3). Note that these heading mnemonics must appear on separate lines immediately following the folio indication for that folio side.

```text
[fol. 105r]
{HD1. DEL SOL}
{HD2. DE UENUS}
text of 105r  (43/1-3)
```

A heading begun in a {HD.} mnemonic (e.g., 3/2) can be continued, after the plus sign, in a {HD1.} mnemonic (e.g., 4/2, and see also Section 3.23).

### 2.6 As was previously noted, the heading may include foliation not adopted by the editor, such as old or non-sequential foliation, foliation in Roman numerals, etc. The retention of such numeration is optional (note its absence in the transcription of Plate 38).


### 2.61 If a folio side contains both a heading and such old folio notation, the folio reference is placed after space/back-slash/space ( \ ) following the text of the heading (e.g., 8/2). If the editor wishes to indicate the presence of several such old folio numbers, space/back-slash/space should be used to separate each number:

```text
{HD. LIBRO + \ 125 \ cxxiij}
```


### 2.62 If a folio side of the manuscript does not have a heading, the heading mnemonic may still be employed to note the presence of old foliation. The heading mnemonic is opened and the folio number is inserted after space/back-slash/space (e.g., 36/2). If more than one such folio  notation is present, space/back-slash/space separates each:

```text
{HD. \ 125 \ cxxii j}
```

It should be noted that if the old foliation is accompanied by some indication such as Folio, or an abbreviation such as Fo. or f., these do not constitute headings and should, in fact, be omitted from the transcription.

## 3. COLUMN BOUNDARIES


### 3.1 The body of all texts is contained in one or more columns on the folio side; even when the text is written across the folio  from one margin to the other, this is taken to be one column. Therefore, the mnemonic {CB.}, which is used to represent the division of the text into columns on the manuscript, must always be used, even if there is only one column of text per side. The number after the mnemonic indicates the type of column format present, not the order of columns on a folio side; thus, {CB2.} indicates that there are two columns of text present on (that area of) the folio side, not that it is the second column, since columns are not numbered. Columns are always transcribed in a logical sense sequence, as in the following examples:

```text
manuscript   transcription
HHHHHHHHHH  [fol. 3r]
{HD. HHHHHHHHHH}
xxxx yyyy   {CB2.
xxxx yyyy   xxxx
xxxx yyyy   xxxx
```

xxxx}
```text
{CB2.
```

yyyy
yyyy
yyyy}
```text
manuscript   transcription
xxxx yyyy   {CB2.
xxxx yyyy   xxxx
```

zzzzzzzzzzzz   xxxx}
zzzzzzzzzzzz   {CB2. yyyy
yyyy}
```text
{CB1.
```

zzzzzzzzzzzz
zzzzzzzzzzzz}
Note in the above examples that (1) the heading is outside the body of the text and is thus included within its own {HD.} mnemonic before the {CB.} mnemonic is opened, (2) the {CB.} mnemonic is placed on a separate line preceding those which contain the body of the text, and (3) the closing brace of the {CB.} mnemonic immediately follows the last character contained within that column.
All material in the body of the text, whether original or inserted, together with any mnemonics needed to enclose it, must be included within the {CB.} mnemonic (see Section 3.11 below). All brace-bounded mnemonics begun within a {CB.} mnemonic must close within the same {CB.} mnemonic. (See Section 3.23 for more information on the use of mnemonics.)

### 3.11 Textual material not physically contained within the column boundaries of a manuscript but logically related to it must be transcribed in its logical place in the text within a {CB.} mnemonic.

(1) Chapter numbers which appear in either the left or the right margin are transcribed within the {CB.} mnemonic which contains the corresponding text:
II {IN4.} Qvando Jacob—  (7/5)
III {IN4.} Josep—   (7/98)
This applies to folio references  in tables of contents as well:
cxxxi {IN1.} Dela batalla fecha entre el Emperador Theodosi-
o et Eugenyo el tirano. cxxx[ij]. {RUB. El .viii. libro es delas  (14/18-19)
(2) See Section 3.224a(2) for a discussion of marginal insertions.
(3) See Section 3.239a for glosses.
(4) See Section 3.239b for addenda.

### 3.12 In order to ensure the accurate joining of parts of divided words in the manuscript, a column must never close with an incomplete word, whether that word is divided across folios or across columns on the same folio. Whenever necessary, therefore, the final letters of a word left incomplete by the scribe at the end of a column must be brought back from the following column or folio side and joined to the first segment after a hyphen; the column is then completed, as usual, with the closing brace (e.g., 2/30). Punctuation is also brought back with the final segment of the word, provided that there is no space intervening between the end of the word and the mark of punctuation. The word segment transposed to complete the final word of a column is, of course, not then transcribed at the start of the next column, which will begin in the transcription with the first complete word.


### 3.13 A column is defined as that text enclosed within the braces of a {CB.} mnemonic, and therefore it is normally impossible to enclose one {CB.} mnemonic within another {CB.} mnemonic. However, it may occur that matter clearly subordinate to a column (names of people, or places, for example) is listed in columns within it, and the only way to make this format clear is to embed a set of {CB.}—s in the principal {CB.}. This ensures that the material is presented in the correct order (e.g., 9/15-20).


### 3.14 A single physical feature such as a diagram, or a miniature, normally appears in the manuscript clearly within the limits of a given column and is transcribed within one brace-bounded {CB.} mnemonic (e.g., 3/3-4). On rare occasions, however, a physical feature is found which does not coincide with the boundaries of a single column because of its irregular shape. Consider the following examples, in which d represents a diagram, and m a miniature:

xxxx xxxx  yyyymmmmmmmmmmmmm
xxxx xxxx  yyyymmmmmmmmmmmmm
ddddddddddd  yyyyyy  mmmmmm
ddddddddddd  yyyyyy  mmmmmm
yyyyyyydddd  yyyyyy  zzzzzz
yyyyyyyyyyy  yyyyyy  zzzzzz
In these cases, the feature should be enclosed within the {CB.} mnemonic which most closely corresponds to its shape; thus the diagram is transcribed as part of the {CB1.} since it spans the whole folio, whereas the miniature is considered part of the {CB2.} format.
```text
{CB2.   (CB2.
```

xxxx   yyyy
xxxx}   yyyy
```text
{CB2.   yyyyyy
```

xxxx   yyyyyy
xxxx}   yyyyyy
```text
{CB1.   yyyyyy
{DIAG.}  {CB2.
```

yyyyyyy   {MIN.}
yyyyyyyyyyy}  zzzzzz
zzzzzz}

### 3.15 If a column contains no text for any reason, a complete, empty {CB.} mnemonic should be used to indicate its position relative to the other columns on that folio side (e.g., 10/12). A totally blank folio side  is represented only by the folio  indication: clearly no column format is present. (See Section 1.23.)


### 3.21 Character Set


### 3.211 Alphabet

The orthography of the manuscript is respected, using the corresponding character from the machine-readable character set where possible. Although the transcription is intended to be as close a rendering of the manuscript text as possible, the limitations of this character set have imposed the following norms:
(1) Capital R, whatever its size relative to other letters and regardless of its position in the word, will be transcribed as upper case R (e.g., 24/50; 33/30). Note that if the manuscript uses rr- in word-initial position, that graph should be respected (e.g., 16/5, 8 & 22; 32/3).
(2) The two common graphs representing capital M,  and , are both transcribed as upper case M, regardless of the position in the word of the graph (e.g., 33/17, 22 & passim).
m1
m2
(3) A distinction is made between the scribal graphs for minuscule i and j, based on the length of the descender. If the graph does not descend below the scribed line it is transcribed i; if the stroke continues below the line, with or without a curve, the letter is transcribed j (e.g., be<n>iamj<n> 7/60; 42/passim).
(4) In the transcription of b, u, and v, care should be taken to distinguish between the scribal graphs for these letters. They should be recorded exactly as they appear in the manuscript; no attempt should be made at standardization based on modern usage (e.g., 2/16 & 36; 20/10; 32/11)
(5) Ampersand is transcribed &. The capital ampersand is distinguished from it by the addition of an apostrophe: &'. (See Plate 6 for examples of each.)
(6) C cedilla, sigmas and palatal n (/—/) are all dealt with in Section 3.215, as are the graphs ch, x, and y with diacritics. For a single n used to represent /—/, see Section 3.211(9) below.
(7) Capital F is represented in many manuscripts by what appears to be a digraph. Despite the paleographic tradition of rendering this symbol as a single character in transcription, consideration
of the phonetic nature of what might be represented in medieval Spanish by f and ff makes it more advisable to represent this digraph in DOSL transcriptions by Ff, that is, an upper case and lower case character. For an example, see 42/26.
(8) If the editor believes that the difference between s and ( (—long s—) in a particular text is significant, he may represent them in the transcription, respectively, as s and s~. The distinction is, however, optional, and editors should refrain from making it particularly in early printed texts where the distribution of the two characters is predictable.
(9) In medieval texts, palatal n (/—/) and l (/./) are occasionally represented by single n and l respectively, rather than the more traditional nn (—) and ll. If it is clear in a particular text, on the basis of frequency of appearance, that this is regular usage and not scribal error, it must be respected in the transcription; that is, a second l, n, or tilde is not to be inserted editorially. If, on the other hand, it is merely a case of scribal omission, the editor should insert the missing part of the graph. This insertion will be a tilde in texts whose transcription regularly requires n~ (see 32/29, where the tilde is inserted by the editor, since the scribe regularly uses the graph —, as may be seen from the form enga—ado on the same line) or <n> in the transcription of earlier texts where /—/ is regularly represented by nn. In the latter case, where a scribe has erroneously copied pena for penna (i.e., pe—a), the editor will transcribe pen[<n>]a. See Section 3.215b.

### 3.212 Numerals

The character set includes the Arabic numerals 0 through 9. Arabic numerals appear chiefly in the folio  designations and rarely in the texts of the manuscripts. Care should be taken by the editor not to use the letters l or O when the numerals 1 or 0 are required, since for computer processing purposes these are quite different characters.
Of course, the appropriate upper or lower case letters are used to represent Roman numerals. The scribe will usually separate Roman numerals from the surrounding text by placing periods immediately before and after them:
estuuo alli tres o .iiij<<o>>. dias  (24/62)
```text
cue<n>tan q<ue> .lxxv. p<er>sonas entraron (7/83)
```

Care should be taken not to insert a space between the first period and the numeral.
The numeral thousand, as well as being represented by the letter M. is occasionally (but with increasing frequency from the sixteenth century onwards) represented by a variety of calder—n which generally takes the form . This character should be expanded in the transcription as <mil>; since it is usually accompanied by a Roman numeral indicating a multiple, ij , for example, will be transcribed as ij <mil>. In the case that multiples are represented in the manuscript by multiple occurrences of the calder—n, the transcription should contain multiple instances of the expansion, thus:   will be transcribed as <mil> <mil>.
mil
mil
mil
mil

### 3.213 Special Characters

The following table contains a list of symbols used in the transcription to represent certain features:
{ }
 braces
 enclose all mnemonics
[  ]
 brackets
 enclose folio reference and all insertions
< >
 angular (broken) brackets
 enclose expansion of scribal abbreviation
<< >>
 double angular (broken) brackets
 enclose characters appearing in the text in a raised or superscript position. (See Section 3.222b.) It should be noted that this does not refer to later interlinear insertions, which are dealt with in Section 3.224a.
( )
 parentheses
 enclose all deletions
\
 back-slash
 precedes old folio notation included in the heading mnemonic (see Section 2.6)
^
 caret
 appears between brackets, where it indicates scribal insertion, or parentheses, where it indicates scribal deletion. For the use of the caret to indicate an orthographic accent mark in the text, sec Section 3.215c.
#
 crosshatch
 preceded by an Arabic numeral, it indicates the presence of a scribal insertion in a non-original hand. See Section 3.224a. For the use of the crosshatch to indicate an orthographic accent mark in the text, see Section 3.215c.
=
 vector (equals sign)
 indicates the relationship of a diagram or a miniature to accompanying text
|
 single vertical bar internal.
 denotes physical divisions separating segments of text (in a diagram, etc.). The single vertical bar is also used to separate items in the identification of the work at the beginning of the transcription (see Section 0.1).
*
 asterisk
 marks reconstruction of illegible text
$
 dollar sign
 is used to indicate that the following (type) character has been editorially deleted because it was inverted in the text (See Section 3.225c.)
@
 at sign
 indicates the presence of an orthographic accent mark on the preceding character. See Section 3.215c.
[+]
 plus sign in square brackets
 is used to link a prefix to its lexical base when they are written separately in the text. See Section 3.221a(5).
+
 plus sign
 is used when the logical reading flow of text contained within a mnemonic is interrupted. The plus sign therefore indicates that the logical continuation of a textual passage delimited by a given mnemonic is to be found in the text delimited by the next like mnemonic. The plus sign, when
used in this fashion, must always  be  preceded  by a space (+). See below for more details.
This use of the plus sign perhaps requires further explanation. Running heads spanning the verso and recto of two consecutive folios and discontinuous rubrics differentiated in the manuscript from surrounding text by their red ink require special coding in any transcription scheme which hopes to represent physical as well as textual features of medieval books. As already indicated, the purpose of the plus sign is to signal that the logical reading flow of text contained within a mnemonic is disrupted. It signals to the reader that the logical continuation of the text is to be found within the very next like mnemonic in the transcription. The editor should pay special attention to cases in which brace-bounded text, such as rubric text, appears physically separated at the end of one column and at the beginning of the following column. If the editor establishes that both portions of text belong to the same rubric, the plus sign should be inserted at the end of the first column in order to indicate the physical interruption. If, on the other hand, the editor decides (on the basis of criteria such as content or punctuation) that the rubrics are two separate entities, no plus sign should be inserted, since there is no physical interruption of the text within a rubric. See Section 2, Heading, and Section 3.231b & c for further explanation and examples of the use of the plus sign in transcription.

### 3.214 Punctuation

a) The following marks of punctuation are used in the transcription:
period   .
colon   :
semi-colon  ; ./ /.
question mark  ?
virgule   /
hyphen   -
calder—n  %
```text
calder—n two  %2
calder—n three  %3
```

double parentheses ((  ))
b) Periods, colons and semicolons may function similarly in medieval manuscripts. (See Section 3.212 for special use of the period with Roman numerals and Section 3.222g for the elimination in transcription of periods which are used to signal scribal abbreviations.) Periods mark textual divisions which do not necessarily coincide with modern sentence divisions, and at times seem to indicate divisions between breath groups. Scribes use two forms of the semi-colon: the chronologically earlier form, , is to be represented by the modern semi-colon; the later form appears as a slash either preceded or followed by a period and is transcribed as /. or ./ (e.g., 20/54, 41/passim).
punto-coma
The editor should take care to distinguish between true punctuation marks and marks introduced merely to fill out the remaining space at the end of a line in order to create an even right margin. Marks used for line filler, although they may resemble punctuation marks (particularly semi-colons) or even letters, should not be transcribed. Examples of lines terminating in line filler of this sort may be found on Plates 12, 25 and 34.
c) Since scribal practice in the matter of word hyphenation is whimsical, line-end hyphens in the transcription will always be an editorially supplied mark, intended to link two parts of a word together, and not intended to reflect actual scribal marks. Since they are editorial in nature, hyphens should never be
inserted by means of brackets or deleted by means of parentheses. On Plate 39, for example, although no mark resembling a hyphen exists in the original text at these points, hyphens have been transcribed at the ends of some lines (e.g., 5, 7, 11, 16, 21) to join parts of a word together. No attempt should be made to reproduce the hyphen—or marks which appear to be hyphens—provided by the scribe on the manuscript. These may aid the editor in deciding if a hyphen is needed, but they should be ignored when irrelevant (e.g., 39/17). The editor should respect the scribal practice of word separation as it occurs in the body of the text. For example, on Plate 1 both en el (line 8) and enel (line 15) occur: since en and el need not, therefore, be written as one word, a hyphen should not be used to join them at the end of line 12. (See Section 3.221c & d for further examples of use of the hyphen.)
d) The virgule—s function is usually the same as that of the modern comma, and it appears in the manuscript as either a vertical bar or an angular slash. The editor may use his own discretion in the use of a blank space  before or after the virgule (see 32/passim; 16/50 & 77).
e) In medieval texts the question mark may be used either as a mark of interrogation or as an exclamation mark, but it is always transcribed as a question mark (38/25 & 31). (See Section 3.226 for the use of double question marks in the transcription of passages of illegible text.)
```text
f) (1) A calder—n, — (e.g., 7/passim), indicates divisions of text which may be akin to paragraphs. It is to be represented by a percent symbol, and must be separated from preceding and/or following text by single spaces. A calder—n may have been supplied by means of scribal insertion, [^%] (e.g., 11/28-32). A calder—n may also be editorially inserted if it is determined that blank space left in the text was intended for a calder—n which was never supplied: [%]. An inserted calder—n (scribal or editorial) is also separated from preceding and/or following text by single spaces. Often the calder—n marks the introduction of rubric text; if the editor considers this to be the function of a particular calder—n, it is to be represented in the transcription as part of the rubric, and should therefore appear within the rubric mnemonic. Editors should be aware, however, that a calder—n followed by rubric text is not necessarily part of that rubric: in Plate 40, for example, the text contains a series of Arabic rubrics, each preceded by a calder—n; a calder—n is also used, however, to introduce the intervening sections of Spanish text. The editor has therefore concluded that the function of the calder—n in this text is not to mark the insertion of a rubric, but rather to separate Arabic and Spanish sections, and has consequently transcribed the % symbol outside of the {RUB.} mnemonic.
(2) The scribal mark used to indicate that the text which follows it constitutes the concluding portion of the previous line is transcribed as %2, and is known for our purposes as calder—n two; it is separated from any preceding or following text by a single space. In the manuscript it may take various forms, including that of a calder—n, but if its function is that of a calder—n two it is to be transcribed %2 regardless of its physical appearance. Normally, however, it appears thus:  (e.g., 4/61 & 70).
```

calderon2
```text
%2 occasionally separates non-rubric text from rubric text present on the same line (e.g., 12/12, 39 & 53). Note again that the %2 symbol should be placed before the rubric mnemonic, not within it.
If %2 is not present in the manuscript it should be editorially inserted after intervening text to show that text which follows to the end of that manuscript line constitutes the concluding portion of the previous line (e.g., 1/23). This is usually only necessary where the relationship between the disjunctive segments of text is not indicated by {RUB.} codes.
In cases where all that follows a %2 is part of a word from the preceding line, and that part is taken up and joined to the rest of the word with a hyphen in the transcription, the %2 is left at the end of the line to mark the original physical lay-out. If there is no %2 in the manuscript, one should be editorially inserted (e.g., 1/32). See also Section 3.231b.
A %2 may also be scribally inserted: [^%2].
(3) Calder—n three is the term applied to the manuscript mark, similar in appearance to % and %2, which signals that the text which follows it to the end of the manuscript line constitutes the concluding portion of the following line. It is represented by the symbol %3 (e.g., 14/12), and is separated from any preceding or following text by a single space. A %3 may be inserted editorially or scribally: [%3] [^%3]. An inserted %3 must be separated from any preceding or following text by single spaces.
```

g) Punctuation should be supplied only where its absence can be interpreted as a case of accidental scribal omission. With the exception of the hyphen, which is always considered an editorial mark anyway, editorially inserted punctuation must appear within square brackets.
h) The parentheses which appear in early printed texts, as in some editions of Celestina, either for purposes of punctuation, or to surround the names of characters and separate them from the dialogue, should be represented in the transcription. Since single parentheses are regularly used to surround text which is deleted, either scribally or editorially, those parentheses which appear in the text are to be represented in the transcription by double parentheses:  ((  )). See Plate 45/passim for examples.

### 3.215 Diacritics

a) The Apostrophe
(1) The addition of a single apostrophe serves to distinguish between c and c cedilla:
cielo / c'ielo; Ciento / C'iento
(2) If desired, the apostrophe may also be used to indicate the presence of a sigma, which may be interpreted phonetically as one of three sibilants in medieval Spanish. If the sigma functions as a c cedilla, it is transcribed c'' (e.g., 32/35); the second apostrophe signals that a sigma and not a c cedilla appears in the manuscript. Likewise, s' or z' is transcribed if the sigma—s function is either s or z (e.g., 32/23-25 & 47). The editor may simply opt to interpret the sigma as one of the medieval sibilants without signaling them as sigmas through use of the apostrophe (e.g., 41/22, 39, 43, 59; the transcription of final s in Plate 37/passim), though for purposes of possible later linguistic analysis of the text, it is always preferable to mark the sigma as such in the transcription.
(3) It will be noted that in the transcription of Plates 46 and 47, e' has also been used to represent the e of the Latin text. Since e is to be considered as a character with a diacritic, rather than an abbreviation for the diphthong ae, its transcription here has been assimilated to that of —.
b) The Tilde
If the digraph nn is never used in the manuscript being edited to represent the palatal — (/—/), then — should be transcribed as n followed immediately by a tilde, n~ (e.g., 20/6 & 7), since our machine-readable
transcription does not permit overprinting of diacritics. If the digraph nn is used, however infrequently, for the palatal in a particular manuscript, the tilde should always be treated as a scribal suppression mark for a following it, i.e., as n<n> (e.g., 6/64). For a single n used to represent /—/, see Section 3.211(9).
Note that when a tilde or similar mark appears in a manuscript above the letter m in the form com~o, it is to be expanded as a geminate, thus: com<m>o in the transcription (16/31, 20/4, 32/30, 37/4).
It is not uncommon to find that the graphs ch and x, representing the palatal phonemes /c/ and /—/ respectively, were accompanied by a diacritic sign resembling a tilde; this practice is found even in texts predating Nebrija—s pronouncement on its advisability. The diacritic should be transcribed as a tilde immediately following the ch or the x. The same practice may be extended to the transcription of any other graphs representing palatals which are accompanied by a tilde or struck through in the text:
```text
que fue amor /. q<ue> much~o ame  41/4
```

& es fech~o E fundado   41/46
ech~ar     47/10
debax~o    46/11
dex~ar     47/39
In some manuscripts, the letter y also appears to bear a diacritic mark, though in many cases the mark is misleading, since it is merely part of a variant scribal tracing of the letter itself. If, however, the editor believes that in a particular manuscript the scribe is consistently using more than one type of y, in order, say, to distinguish the representation of a consonantal sound from that of a vocalic sound, he may transcribe the letter clearly written with a diacritic as y~. Note, for instance, that the difference between the y of ynperial (line 40) and that of muy (line 42) is omitted in the transcription of the text in Plate 41, because no consistent distinction is being made: the word rrey in line 6 carries the mark, but Rey in line 35 does not; the stroke above the y in Plate 6/passim and in Plate 23/passim is regularly used in the tracing of that letter, and so does not appear in the transcription either. In general, the variant tracings of the letter are insignificant.
Editors should take care to distinguish between true diacritics and the ligatures which sometimes appear over the nexus nt, and occasionally others, such as nd or ng. Ligatures do not have to be represented in the transcription: editors should therefore ignore them entirely or, alternatively, transcribe them as otiose tildes; ligatures should never be expanded as if a suppressed nasal consonant were involved. In Plate 16, for instance, the writer of the glosses (lines 31-50 and 59-77) included many otiose tildes over nasal consonants; the tildes are included in the transcription between parentheses (lines 31, 33, 36, 61, 62, 64, etc.). For the sake of consistency in the transcription, the similar mark over the nt nexus of rrecuen(~)ta (1. 60), rrazonamjen(~)tos (1. 63), and en(~)tro (1. 68), has been represented in the same way, though it would also have been acceptable for the editor to ignore it and transcribe rrecuenta, rrazonamjentos, and entro. In no case should the mark be expanded as a nasal consonant; en<n>tro would therefore be unacceptable. In transcribing texts in which the scribe has made liberal use of ligatures, it is recommended that the editor should ignore them, once it has been established that they are indeed otiose marks.
c) Orthographic Accent Marks
Orthographic accent marks (excluding the tilde from this category) appear only sporadically in Spanish medieval texts, and their use varies from one document to another sometimes (more commonly in Latin texts than in Romance ones) they indicate the position of the tonic stress, but they are also used to indicate, for example, an orthographic geminate, or to discern between homographs. In order to indicate in the transcription the presence of an orthographic accent, the editor should insert, immediately following the
letter that is accented in the text, the symbols @' (i.e., the apostrophe) for an acute accent, @' for a grave accent, @^ for a circumflex, and @# for a dieresis:
```text
manuscript  transcription
```

s——llos   se@'e@'llos
onrra   onr@'r@'a
—lguno   a@'lguno
—   o@^
—   a@#
confess—  confesso@' (46/19)
gast—   gaste@'  (46/30)
(It is unimportant that within electronic text processing, the ASCII grave accent may be interpreted for printing purposes as an opening single quotation mark: '.) It is of course crucial that the editor recognize the difference between these diacritics and suppression marks (see Section 3.222).

### 3.22 Basic Editorial Operations


### 3.221 Word Separation

The orthography of the scribe should be respected, within, of course, the limitations imposed by the character set (see Section 3.21). The spacing between words in a given manuscript is part of the scribal orthographic practice (and the physical characteristics of his materials) with which the editor must become familiar. The editor should attempt to reproduce what he perceives to be the scribal pattern of word separation and conjunction except as documented below. In cases where it is unclear whether the scribe—s intention was to write forms as a single word or as two, the editor should attempt to establish a pattern in the scribe—s orthography on the basis of the same or similar occurrences in the rest of the text (or at least, that part of it written in the same hand), and follow that pattern. In those cases that remain unclear, the editor should always, while respecting the guidelines which follow, attempt to reproduce as closely as possible what appears in the text.
It must be stressed that these guidelines are designed to serve essentially as orientation for editors; word separation in Spanish manuscripts and early printed works is not amenable to general principles which can be applied on all occasions. Moreover, the frequently uneven disposition of letters makes it difficult for editors to decide whether spacing is intentional or an accidental reflection of the scribe—s attempt to fill a line or compress more text into it. Editors must often therefore exercise judgment on the basis of what they believe is the tendency in a particular text, rather than search for hard and fast rules concerning the separation of specific forms. Traditional philological practice has left considerable liberty for editors to represent word separation in the way they feel most suitable for their readers; thus a medieval text edited for a modern non-specialist readership will to a great extent adjust the word separation of the original to match that of modern orthography. In the preparation of transcriptions for DOSL, however, it is more important to preserve as closely as possible the appearance of the original text, while at the same time striving to create useful dictionary entries and concordances. Since these goals will sometimes produce conflicting interpretations about whether or not a particular combination of forms should be separated editorially, editors should bear in mind one overriding principle: if the norms below are not found to provide satisfactorily for a specific case, the editor may separate any combination of elements in accordance with modern orthographical practice provided that the separation is indicated in the transcription by the editorial insertion of a space, that
is, by [ ]. This flexibility notwithstanding, editors are strongly encouraged to become familiar with the norms that follow.
In cases of potential editorial intervention, it is important to recognize the distinction between function forms (in general terms, those which serve to indicate grammatical relationships) and lexical items (which carry the semantic content of the text), since function forms, being largely, though not exclusively, atonic, tend to be conjoined to tonic lexical items in medieval scribal practice. For the purpose at hand, the category of function forms includes prepositions, conjunctions, relatives, indefinite and personal pronouns (both clitic and disjunctive); determiners in general, such as articles, demonstratives, preposed possessive adjectives, otro, todo, cada, tal; and auxiliary verbs. Lexical items include the traditional major categories of noun, adjective, main verb, and adverb. The editor should take care to distinguish a true preposition from a prefix; the distinction can often only he made on a case-by-case basis, since the two may be formally identical (see below, Section 3.221a(5)). The second criterion the editor must attempt to apply is the scribe—s intention: whether the separation visible in the text reflects the scribe—s regular practice, or whether it was imposed in that particular instance by the physical characteristics of the text or of the material upon which it was written. Transcription of what is believed to be the scribe—s regular practice will be discussed in section (a); other, accidental, cases will be discussed in section (b). Sections (c) and (d) deal with the special case of words left incomplete at the end of a manuscript line or before other textual breaks.
a) Regular word separation.
(1) Function form plus function form. The joining or separation of two (or more) function forms should be reflected in the transcription exactly as it appears in the text. Function forms which are written together in the text should not be separated editorially, regardless of whether elision has taken place:
del    della
enel    parati
assus    loque
enque    niella
delos    ensu
ami    convn
poruma    consu
eneste    antel
sobrel    poral
poresso    sino
poren (5/13)   desuuyr sea & uaziar sa (15/46)
cadauno (5/42)   yl Recibio bien (6/16)
cercal (7/15)   aotro (8/6)
todesso (11/50)   es nombre proprio dessisse (11/73)
una sobrotra (17/18)  perder san los aueres (15/45)
entressi (23/49)   porassi (23/64)
de tod entodo (38/40)  fijo besar te (38/44)
tomo sende (24/5)  sieruo dalguno (25/85)
Whenever the two function forms appear separately in the text, the separation must be respected in the transcription, regardless of what the editor perceives the scribe—s regular preference in such cases to be.
Among the function forms, those that editors report most difficulty with are the conjunctions. Given their grammatical status as markers of constituent boundaries within a text, they have often been extracted from orthographical combinations by editors and made to stand alone. Editors who wish to carry out this practice may do so, as long as the combination does not involve elision of any segment, and provided that, as always, the inserted space is represented by [ ]. Thus the example niella which appears in the list above may alternatively be transcribed as ni[ ]ella; further examples include si[ ]le, e[ ]si, ni[ ]lo, ni[ ]si, y[ ]ellos, o[ ]lo, etc. In the list above, however, yl Recibio bien does not have an alternative transcription, since the pronoun shows elision of the vowel; examples such as nil dixo nada, sil viesse, el firio fall into the same category. The combination sino / si no is a special case: it must always be transcribed in the way it appears in the text; no attempt should be made to separate or join its segments in imitation of modern orthographical practice.
Respecting the union of function forms as it appears in texts naturally extends to subordinating elements such as porque, paraque, anteque, puesque, and so on, written without spacing by the scribe. Even in complex prepositional phrases and subordinators containing nouns, editors may choose not to insert spacing if they believe, on the basis of their familiarity with the text, that the orthographic combination is in keeping with the general tendency of that text. In the transcription of Plate 28, for example, no space is inserted editorially in deguisa que (line 13) because this orthography is the scribe—s regular practice: it occurs again in line 48. On the other hand, en guisa que is always written as three words (lines 38, 43, 56), and the scribe—s spacing is respected in the transcription. In Plate 41, the scribe—s spacing is clearly constrained by his attempt to produce lines of similar length, causing the phrase a guis(sa) de to appear sometimes without a space between the first two elements (lines 55 and 79), and sometimes with a space (lines 63 and 71). The editor has therefore chosen to insert a space in the former cases, and in the similar phrase a manera de (line 65). This serves to illustrate the importance of the editor—s awareness of scribal practice in particular cases, and how it may lead to different, though equally valid, decisions. Once again, however, it should be emphasized that this type of intervention is left to the editor—s discretion, as long as spaces inserted or deleted are duly marked as such in the transcription. See also Section 3.221b below.
(2) Function form plus lexical item. In the interest of sorting lexical items to he culled for dictionary purposes, when scribal practice is to join function forms as proclitics to items of lexical substance, DOSL practice requires that they be transcribed separately in order for the lexical items to appear appropriately alphabetized in concordance entries. The separation should be indicated editorially by the insertion of one space enclosed in square brackets:
entro Jacob a Egipto — a[ ]ueer  (6/8-11)
```text
comenc'o a[ ]cuedar en aq<ue>lla yda (6/54)
```

correncia de[ ]sa<n>gre   (9/43)
el s<er>ie a[ ]xativa   (24/27)
es esta la figura de[ ]uenus  (33/3)
me[ ]dio su /. vanda   (41/12)
This editorial separation should take place even in cases where the shape of the function form has been altered by processes such as assimilation or elision, or where the orthography of the lexical item has been affected (e.g., the gemination of certain word-initial consonants) by the scribal practice of welding them together:
sube muger con uerga d[ ]oro  (1/63)
grandes fechos d[ ]armas   (14/20)
tabla d[ ]estanno    (15/5)
alu<n>brad m[ ]agor<<a>>  (41/62)
en l[ ]ascendent
te vas alla a[ ]ssentar al rincon  (22/30-31)
de[ ]rrey o de princep
In the latter case, the alteration in orthography represents an attempt on the part of the scribe to reflect the pronunciation of the words involved (e.g., gemination of the s to indicate its voiceless nature).
An exception to this practice of editorial intervention is the case in which a nexus of one of the prepositions a, de, en and a lexical item creates an adverbial phrase; here, the orthography of the text should be respected in all cases in the transcription, and no editorial separation made even when the nexus is written together. The reason for this is that in some cases the viability of the lexical item outside the adverbial construction is questionable (e.g., en balde, a sabiendas), and even when this is not the case, it may well be linguistically significant that the scribe felt the expression to be a single entity. Modern academic orthography should not sway the editor—s decisions concerning medieval texts; consistency would require that if, for example, daqui were separated editorially, donde would have to undergo the same treatment, since both aqui and onde were viable elements in the medieval language. A certain arbitrariness is still present in modern orthography; note the spacing in apenas but a menudo, al rev—s but alrededor, en seguida / enseguida, de prisa / deprisa, a prisa / aprisa, etc. It is clear that these adverbials were often perceived by speakers to be unitary elements.
acuestas     (22/23)
aderredor     (25/35-36)
asabyendas    (32/38)
amenudo     (40/75)
fue assoora llena toda la uilla  (38/100-101)
aosadas
adesora
alreues
apriessa
embalde
derrezio
dalli     (7/14)
Naturally, when the function form and lexical item appear written separately in the text, the separation must be respected in the transcription; as always, the overriding consideration will be close representation of the original.
Since word separation is probably the issue that produces most initial uncertainty for editors, it may be instructive to compare the treatment given to specific forms in one text. In the transcription of Plate 15, for example, enella (line 5), del (line 13), enla (line 14), desta (line 26), ensus (line 53) are all left conjoined, in accordance with (1) above, since they are combinations of preposition and pronoun, article, demonstrative, or possessive, all function forms for purposes of transcription. In line 25, loque is left conjoined as a combination of pronoun and relative, and so is sea, a combination of pronoun and auxiliary verb; again, for purposes of transcription, they are all function forms. On the other hand, d[ ]estanno (line 5) is separated editorially in accordance with
(2) above, since it combines a preposition with a noun, the latter a lexical item. Note, however, that no attempt is made to separate adelante (line 62), despite the fact that delante alone may be considered a lexical item. Nor is the decision based on modern Spanish orthographic practice, but rather on the exception to (2) above, in that adelante constitutes an adverbial unit, like acuestas, derrezio or embalde.
(3) Lexical item plus function form. If the function form is joined to the lexical item as an enclitic, no editorial separation should be made, since the lexical form will remain correctly alphabetized for concordance and dictionary purposes.
& mouersa la red    (4/27)
Et llamol. & dixol. Jacob—  (6/74)
& affirmalos bien    (2/33)
saliol a Recebir    (7/99-100)
& ponla entu dedo   (12/33)
& taialo. & echalo fuera   (26/4)
At the same time, the editor should on no account join function forms to lexical items in imitation of modern orthographical practice when the scribe writes them separately:
dezir le
contando gelo
fue se
abaxo le
nos otros
vos otros
(4) Lexical item plus lexical item. The editor should make no attempt to join forms such as the following when the scribe treats them as separate words. Such forms particularly, though not exclusively, include adverbs which originated as morphological compounds of two independently viable lexical items. Again, the overriding consideration must be to respect as far as possible what appears in the text; thus, if such forms appear conjoined in one orthographical unit, they should be transcribed as such; if they appear as two orthographical units, the transcription should reproduce that separation:
toda uia (5/26) / todauia
larga mente / largamente
cumplida mente / cumplidamente
tan bien / tanbien
otro si / otrossi (6/14)
entre tanto / entretanto (24/73)
(5) Prefix plus root. When a prefix (which may sometimes, but not always, be formally identical to a preposition) is not orthographically joined to the root of a morphological compound, once more in the interest of culling lexical elements for DOSL, the editor should insert in the space between them a plus sign enclosed in square brackets; there should be no space on either side of the square brackets, nor between them and the plus sign:
pro[+]meter   de[+]nostados
man[+]levar  man[+]levar
sos[+]tener   inter[+]venir
em[+]bihar   la ante[+]puerta
con[+]plir   a[+]caudillar
en[+]noblescer   a[+]cercar
a[+]cometer   a[+]contescer
con[+]bater   des[+]saborido
des[+]otorgar   mal[+]fechor
en[+]fingidamente (39/26-27)
Justification for this transcription is twofold: first, in the concordances generated from texts, prefixed forms will be identified as distinct from non-prefixed forms (e.g., occurrences of pro meter will not be included with those of meter); second, the orthographic variant of representing the prefixed form as two elements will be preserved and recorded for DOSL use. Note that this use of the plus sign is quite different from that used in Headings and within other mnemonics, described in Sections 2 and 3.23. It is also important to note that this practice is to be followed only in the case of separated prefixes; it must not be used to conjoin adverbs or other forms in which prefixes are not involved, such as rica mente, larga mente in imitation of modern orthography. See 3.221a(4) above.
When prefixed forms are divided on two lines of text, and the division separates the prefix from the root, both the plus sign in brackets and a hyphen must be inserted at the line break. Insertion of [+] alone will cause the prefix and root to appear in the concordance as separate items; insertion of the hyphen alone will omit any indication that the scribe may not have intended the prefix and root to be orthographically joined. Thus the correct transcription in such cases is as follows:
```text
manuscript    transcription
```

e sus enemigos fueron de  e sus enemigos fueron de[+]-
nostados    nostados
For further information on incomplete words at the end of a line, see 3.221c below.
b) Irregular word separation. Certain circumstances sometimes inhibited the scribe—s regular practice of word separation. Two distinct classes of irregular word separation may be encountered, and are described in what follows. The basic guideline for editors is that in case of doubt, the transcription should remain as faithful as possible to the original, and that in all the following cases, spaces inserted or suppressed in the transcription should always be indicated as such by the use of brackets or parentheses, respectively (see Sections 3.223, 3.224, and 3.225 for details of the encoding for deletion and insertion).
(1) The editor may find instances where because of simple misplacement of the pen the scribe has separated word segments which he normally joins. Also, the editor may find words joined together due to scribal misplacement of the pen or to the intentional compacting of words on a manuscript or printed line, for example, to complete a segment of text at the end of a folio side. In such instances the author should feel free to interpret word separation in the light of the conventional scribal pattern apparent in the text, indicating editorial insertion and suppression of space:
```text
manuscript    transcription
```

es laladeza    es la[ ]ladeza
el santoa nno    el santo[ ]a( )nno
&elquisera    &[ ]el[ ]qui[ ]sera
(2) In certain cases the editor may determine that an anomalous instance of word separation is due not to careless pen placement or —crowding— of text but rather to a misinterpretation by the scribe of his source text. In such cases the scribe may produce a grammatically correct but erroneous reading which is not correct in context. The editor should correct the spacing to produce what he believes to be the correct separation, once again indicating editorial insertion and suppression of space:
estas tres sen<n>al( )es sobredichas son
esta que[ ]mando
& tod( )a[ ]uia so
This last transcription would occur in a text which consistently presents toda uia written as separate elements.
c) Incomplete words at the end of a line. Words that are split onto two lines in a manuscript are normally represented in the transcription in the same way, with a hyphen inserted (editorially) at the end of the first line (e.g., 1/11, 15, 17). Here again, however, it may be crucial for the editor to establish the regular pattern of scribal usage before deciding on the insertion of a hyphen. Thus in a case such as the following, where the manuscript has
—entraron en
la uilla de tari—
a hyphen should be inserted in the transcription after en if, and only if, all other occurrences of en followed by a definite article in the text are joined as a single word. The existence of other occurrences of the same elements written separately in the text, however, will justify an editorial decision not to insert the hyphen. In the same way, in the following example, no hyphen should be inserted after larga if similar adverbs appear in the text written separately:
—fablaron larga
mente & los caualleros—
```text
When a hyphen is inserted, it is important to note that the divided word must be completed after it at the end of the manuscript line on which it begins if any intervening material would otherwise not allow the completing word segment immediately to follow the hyphen. On Plate 4, lines 79 and 81, a %2 followed by a word which is part of the preceding line intervenes between two parts of a word: in such cases, the second part of the word is brought back and joined to the first part with a hyphen; the computer then sorts it as a single lexical unit, but the hyphen indicates that the second part was brought back from the following line:
{IN1.} Cap<ito>lo .xij[<<o>>]. de cuemo deuen armar la ca<n>-dela %2 signos.
```

pora quemar. & cuemo deuen poner la tabla
```text
{IN1.} Cap<ito>lo .xiij[<<o>>]. de cuemo deuen par-tir %2 de los signos.
```

la tabla de los signos. & figurar la.
For specific information concerning line division which separates a prefix from the rest of a word, see 3.221a(5) above.
d) Other instances in which a divided word must be completed immediately after a hyphen in order to avoid separation of word segments because of intervening data:
(1) Word division at the end of a column (e.g., 24/73). The completing word segment must appear immediately after the hyphen since the necessary closing brace of the {CB.} mnemonic would otherwise destroy the joining signaled by the hyphen. This norm must be adhered to even in the case of parallel columns in which the text does not flow directly from the left-hand column to the right-hand one: in bilingual texts, the left-hand column may always contain text in one language, the right-hand column text in the other (Spanish on the left and Latin on the right, for example). This means that a word broken at the end of a column is completed, not in the immediately following column, but in the corresponding column on the next folio side. The completing segment must nevertheless appear immediately after the hyphen and before the closing brace of the {CB.} mnemonic in the column in which it began. For an example of this format, see Plates 46 and 47.
```text
(2) Word division related to %2, [%2], %3, [%3]. (The function of these punctuation marks was discussed in Section 3.214f.) Part of a word may appear in the manuscript immediately before or after the %2 or %3 (e.g., 4/81; 1/65), and in both cases the divided words must be completed, as in the following example:
manuscript   transcription
```

ttt- xxx-   ttttt-tt xxx-xxx
```text
ttt ttttt %2 xxx xxx  ttttt %2 xxx
```

(3) Word division before any mnemonic (e.g., 15/16). The divided word should be completed after a hyphen before the opening brace of any mnemonic.
(4) Word division before the closing brace of any continued mnemonic (e.g., 12/70). The final item of the text of a mnemonic which closes with the plus sign (a continued mnemonic) cannot be an incomplete word: the word must be completed, after a hyphen, before the mnemonic is closed.
(5) Note that the hyphen should never be enclosed within square brackets. Therefore, in cases where material is supplied by the editor—in a heading for example—the square brackets should be closed before the hyphen and, if necessary, opened again after it:
```text
{HD. GEN[*E]-[*SI]S +}
```


### 3.222 Scribal Abbreviation and Expansion

```text
a) An important feature of scribal orthographic convention is the use of abbreviations to represent frequently repeated letters or groups of letters. Editors will find it helpful when initially perusing the text prior to transcription, to develop an awareness of scribal preferences in the matter of abbreviation. Broken brackets < > are used to enclose those characters supplied editorially as expansions of scribal abbreviations: q<ue>.
b) Superscript characters, those written above the rest of the line of text (not to be confused with interlinear scribal insertion), often accompany scribal abbreviations, and may be a letter or letters from the part of the word suppressed, as in q<u><<i>> (1/34 & 42): the double broken brackets surrounding the letter i
```

denote that it is written above the line (see Section 3.213). The a and the o also frequently occur as superscript letters with accompanying suppressed letters (see, for example, Plate 6/15, 27 & passim.)
Editors should be careful to distinguish between the general suppression mark and true superscript letters; it is easy to confuse the scribal representation of a superscript a, for example, and a bar or tilde (compare, for example, qua<n>to 6/47 with q<u><<a>>nto 6/51). If the editor finds that the superscript a is used as a general mark of suppression, simultaneously with its function as a superscript letter embodying the representation of an additional implied letter, he is at liberty to interpret the mark as the case warrants: for example, the same form may be interpreted as q<u><<a>>l or as q<ue>l, depending on context. Cumbersome deletion/insertion sequences to indicate scribal use of the —wrong— mark of suppression are thus avoided.
Editors should be aware that not all scribal abbreviations are unambiguous; thus while scribes often distinguished between the abbreviations of ver- and vir- by using a suppression mark in the former case and a superscript i in the latter (see, for example, u<er>dad, Plate 7/7), some texts frequently show v traversed by a downstroke used as an abbreviation for both sequences. Expansion of forms abbreviated in this manner should be based on internal evidence: if, for example, the text uses vertud as the unabbreviated form, the abbreviation should be expanded in this way too, rather than as virtud. In the same way, the editor of the transcription reproduced for Plate 4 chose cap<ito>lo (lines 5, 14, 57, 58, 60, etc.) rather than cap<itu>lo for the expansion of the scribal abbreviation since in the text as a whole, the unabbreviated form capitolo was by far the most frequently attested, notwithstanding the appearance of capitulo in lines 7-8.
The transcription of the following common abbreviations should be noted with special care:
xpianos  > xp<<i>><sti>anos
xianos  > x<r><<i>><sti>anos
x.po  > xp<ist>o
ga  > g<r><<a>><cia>
See Section 3.237c for the treatment of the fossilized Greek letters x and p.
c) If it is believed that a scribe has accidentally omitted a suppression mark rather than a character or characters, an editorial insertion of the expansion should be made: q[<ue>]. The editorial brackets surrounding the broken brackets serve to indicate that no scribal indication of suppression of characters is present in the manuscript (e.g., 9/65; 12/31).
d) If a suppression mark occurs over a word in which the letter to be suppressed has in fact been supplied, the editor should add the letter between broken brackets, and delete the redundant letter supplied by the scribe, thus: co<m>(m)passion.
On the other hand, if a suppression mark is truly otiose, the editor may choose either to ignore it entirely or to delete it editorially, provided that he is consistent in his practice (e.g., 16/31, 33, & passim).
e) Scribal convention often continued to use abbreviations of Latin forms to represent their Romance counterparts. When such abbreviations appear in Romance texts (and not as part of a longer Latin passage), they are in a sense orthographic variants of the Romance forms, and should be treated as such by expanding the abbreviation as the vernacular form:
sc.o  > <santo> (23/36)
fc.ta  > <fecha>
pc.to  > <pecado>
Note that the whole of the Spanish form is enclosed in broken brackets, despite any coincidence between individual letters and those of the abbreviation. The expansion <sancto> would, however, be required in a text in which the only unabbreviated form used was sancto. In rare cases, a Latinate abbreviation of this type can form part of a longer Spanish word; here, in order to respect the integrity of the rest of the word, regular expansion of the abbreviation should be carried out. Thus the name Santiago represented in a text as s.ciago would be transcribed as s<an>c<t>iago.
f) The abbreviation  is sometimes found for the syllable con. Despite its formal similarity to an inverted letter c, it is not to be interpreted as such, but rather as a representation of the syllable as a whole. The expansion in the transcription is therefore <con>. For an example, see Plate 13/13. The symbol  is employed as an abbreviation for word-final -us or -os, and is therefore represented in the transcription as <us> or <os>, as appropriate (2/45, 6/9, 7/9, 25/21, 43/47).
co
us-os
g) Some subject matter will have abbreviations peculiar to it. In a treatise on magic, for example, one finds .y., which is expanded as y<magen> (33/8 & 10). Note that the periods which serve here to signal a scribal abbreviation are not to be reproduced in the transcription (for further examples, see 46/32 and 47/6 and 7). When, however, periods used in this way cannot or are not meant to be interpreted, the editor should reproduce them (e.g., fulan .N., 12/7 & passim). Likewise, when the editor deems that a period serves not only to signal an abbreviation, but concurrently as a mark of punctuation, or as a graphic separator of some kind, he is to reproduce it. (See Plate 22, in which the interlocutors in the dialogue are identified in abbreviated terms, e.g., Ce<lestina>. The period here is retained to aid in delineating between the indication of the new speaker and that speaker—s discourse. Thus the period is retained in the same way as when it follows the unabbreviated name of the interlocutor.)
Medical texts make use of certain apothecary symbols carried over from Greek and Latin tradition to abbreviate in prescriptions frequently-occurring weights and measures and other formulas:
```text
manuscript    transcription
```

     <onc'a>, <onc'as>  (37/62)
onza
     <escrupulo>, <escrupulos> (37/63)
escrupulo
    R<ecipe>   (37/77)
recipe
     <dracma>, <dracmas>  (37/77)
dracma
     <medio/a>, <& medio/a> (37/78)
medio
     l<i>b<ra>, l<i>b<ras>
libra
In such medical manuscripts those abbreviations using periods often appear in conjunction with numerals; since periods are retained in numerals (see Section 3.212), when a single period is present between the abbreviation and the numeral, the period is retained for the numeral (e.g., 37/77 & 86).

### 3.223 Deletions: Scribal and Editorial

a) Parentheses are used to indicate text which is to be deleted. It should be noted here that if two elements occur, one of which is redundant, it is general policy to take the first as correct and to delete the second, as in the following examples:
una tabla cuemo la ot-
(t)ra q<ue> esta a parte de dentro (29/24-25)
```text
mas preciado q<ue> el calca-
```

(calca)diz    (17/61-62)
pugnicion de (de) dios   (14/22)
```text
so<n> las q<ue>
```

(las que) fallan    (17/66-67)
It should be observed that this is not the same process as that discussed above in Section 3.222d, where a scribal suppression mark is expanded and a redundant letter following it is deleted.
```text
b) A scribe will normally indicate text to be deleted with a series of dots written beneath the text (punteado) and/or a horizontal stroke through the text (tachado). Erasure and overwriting are also methods of scribal deletion. To signal scribal deletion a caret ^ is placed immediately after the opening parenthesis, and the text follows with no intervening space: (^text); the presence or absence of the caret thus serves to distinguish between scribal and editorial deletions:
uini(^ni)eron   scribal deletion
```

uini(ni)eron   editorial deletion
Because all material coded within parentheses is suppressed during DOSL concordance generation, any material appearing in brackets contained within parentheses will be deleted as well. If he wishes, the editor may emend a word scribally marked for deletion, although the word will not appear in the concordance:
```text
(^p[<r><<i>>]mero)
```

c) Since matter marked for deletion may eventually be eliminated from certain edited states of the text and the corresponding concordances produced from them, it is important that the parentheses signaling deletion be properly positioned in relation to the surrounding text. Text marked for deletion may consist of: part of a word; a complete word or several complete words; a phrase consisting of word segments and complete words; or it may simply be space which has to be deleted. Note the correct transcription for each of the above cases in the examples which follow; the presence or absence of spaces before and after the parentheses should be especially noted, for proper spacing ensures correct word separation.
(1) Word interior letters deleted: no spaces
buen(~)as  (16/33)
```text
uini(^ni)eron
```

(2) Word initial or final letters deleted: space preceding or following parentheses to ensure correct word separation
```text
la (^l)una  (15/18)
ymagen(^es) poca (2/13)
```

(3) Complete words deleted: spaces preceding and following parentheses to ensure correct word separation
```text
el (^el) fondon  (4/71)
```

& que (que) quando (17/10)
(4) Words and word segments deleted: because all material coded within parentheses is suppressed for concordance purposes, spaces both within and outside the parentheses of a deleted sequence must be calculated to result in the proper word separation:
transcription    concordance
```text
troba(^ndo los) los  = troba los
```

segun(guna fi)da figura  = segunda figura
las dos pla-
(dos pla)netas   = las dos planetas
It will be noted that in order to ensure that word fragments are properly joined in the concordance, where the completing portion of the word is joined to a deleted element (as in the last two examples) it is necessary to ensure that all extraneous spaces also be deleted. Where a word breaks at the end of a line (as in the last example) the editor must be sure to supply a hyphen as well (see also 17/5-6).
Because all material coded within parentheses is suppressed for concordance purposes, if a space included within the parentheses of a deletion sequence is the only one separating two words, a compensatory space must be provided by the editor to ensure correct word separation:
transcription    concordance
```text
su(^s bis) auuelo(^s)  = su auuelo
```

(5) Space deleted: a space is left within the parentheses
```text
tal (^ )es opinion(^ )es del Rey
```


### 3.224 Insertions: Scribal and Editorial

There are many kinds of textual material added by scribes after the production of the original text. Editors should establish into which of the following three categories such additional material falls:
—
 A. Scribally Inserted Emendations: Marginal and interlinear insertions added with the intention of correcting faulty copying, or supplying missing material (dealt with in the following Section, 3.224a).
—
 B. Glosses: Explanations of the content of the original text (see Section 3.239a).
—
 C. Addenda: Later expansions of the content of the original text (see Section 3.239b).

### 3.224a Scribally Inserted Emendations

```text
All insertions, whether scribal or editorial, marginal or interlinear, are bounded by brackets: [ ]. Scribal insertions are distinguished from editorial insertions (Section 3.224d) by the addition of a caret immediately after the opening bracket in the former case: [^] (e.g., 2/27). Text follows the caret immediately, with no intervening space.
```

The text of a scribal insertion, which appears in brackets preceded by a caret, normally constitutes a correction or completion of the original text:
```text
en la[^s] p<ro>priedades que an  (17/31)
```

The editor may override a scribal insertion by enclosing the insertion in parentheses:
```text
([^que])
```

If the editor wishes to delete a portion of a more lengthy scribal insertion, he should embed parentheses within the brackets which mark the scribal insertion:
```text
[^xxx (que) xxx]
```

The editor should note carefully the following norms for the transcription of inserted text:
(1) When dealing with insertions (scribal or editorial) proper spacing must be left before and/or after the inserted material. Failure to provide such spacing can seriously affect word separation in any concordance produced from the text. Note the role played by spaces in the transcription of the following insertions:
(a) Word interior letters inserted: no space
enel ascen[den]te (17/45)
fig[<ur>]ras  (9/65)
(b) Word initial or final letters inserted: space preceding or following brackets to ensure correct word separation
```text
& [^a]matala en  (2/36)
```

por la[s] horas  (18/45)
(c) Complete words inserted: spaces preceding and following brackets to ensure correct word separation
```text
en [^cuerno] de buey (15/59)
```

(d) A combination of words and word segments inserted: space preceding or following brackets (if necessary) to ensure correct word separation
et la[s casas] son space needed following bracket
```text
otra [^ma]nera  no space needed following bracket
```

(e) Space inserted: a space is left within the brackets
```text
ala[^ ]ladeza de (19/41)
```

(See Section 3.221 for an important discussion of editorial insertion and deletion of space.)
(2) If a marginal insertion consists of more than one line of text it should be transcribed line by line, as it appears in the manuscript; however, the final inserted line should run on to the main body of the text, as in examples 1 & 2 below. The examples indicate the three possible positions of occurrence of marginal insertions and the proper transcription for each.
